        - Thought through and not drawn: through the glass PC, which would fill with the stream's light. The PC would have to move to the desk's near end, where the trestle stands in front of it, and the stream would come out under the desk in the middle of the rows of skills.
        - The fuller draft at `/lab/skills-about-draft` opens on the lamp, with a switch back to the front edge.
        - If he picks the lamp, the bench keeps its bronze angle as part of the desk, and the third call's "a bronze front edge that carries the stream" becomes "a bronze front edge".
    - **For the kit, from his words:** everything on the desk is made to the bar, and the things he named are where the rough draft is furthest from it: the keyboard and the mouse, the camera, and the PC most of all. The PC's glass stays.
30. **What Skills and About say (Nov 14). He asked for Fable's read of his résumé. Nothing here is ruled, and the words are his.**
    - His words are in Settled, Words: "that's probably something that we should discuss".
    - Fable's read, said to him:
        - The résumé makes one argument, and it is the positioning line in Settled, Words, almost word for word: he takes a product from the first conversation with a client to production, and proves it with evaluation.
        - Evaluating and red teaming frontier models is now a large part of it. On the site it is one list under AI Product Engineering, and the live About, written in July, does not mention it.
        - The site's Skills makes a different argument: four pillars named in July, with the résumé's six groups poured into them.
    - **For Skills, two things to decide:**
        - The shape of the rows. Keep the four pillars, or cut the rows along the path a product takes through him: understand it, design it, build it, prove it. The same lists in four rows, and the section then says his positioning without a sentence of copy. Fable's pick: the path. It would change Open 6 and what he approved in the second round, the résumé's six groups under his four skill sets.
        - The length. The 77 are the résumé's list, which is written to be matched by keyword. Some pairs say one thing twice, as "Generative AI" beside "LLMs" and "AI Agents" beside "Agentic AI". To an engineer reading the site they read as padding. Fable's suggestion: the résumé's list less its doubles, about 60, still more than the live site's 48.
    - **For About:**
        - The three tabs are his structure, and the live text was not written for them. Dealt out, the first tab has two paragraphs and the other two have one each. Each tab needs its own text.
        - To be put right in the text as it stands: "Twelve years" and "40+ clients" (Settled, Words), the tense of the line about his studio, and no mention of the evaluation work.
        - What the résumé cannot say is what About is for: why he builds, what he wants to do, what he plans.
    - One thing above both sections: the résumé's title and the hero's "AI Product Engineer" are different (Open 1). The hero is worked last, and the title still decides how Skills and About are worded.
    - He rewrites the copy himself. Fable drafts only if he asks.
    - **His answer (Nov 14), in Settled, Skills and About:** the four skill sets stay, each row is cut to its name and its lists, the lists are cut to the big ones with a game development group added, and About has two tabs with placeholder text for now. He did not take up rows that follow a product's path. Fable's first cut of the lists is in Open 28, the fifth round. Which skills stay, and the words of both tabs, are his.

    - **The derby of six, built on his word (Nov 14). In front of him. Nothing picked.** At `/lab/skill-things`, Study 24, built in the web chat. Rough 3D, two things for each row, the recommended one on top:
        - AI Product Engineering: the CPU opened up (a lid, a lit die, its base and its contacts standing apart, turning), and the CPU in its socket on a square of board with lit traces.
        - Full-Stack Engineering: a screen with its back off, which the cursor turns right round, and a tower in section with lit rooms on six floors.
        - Design Systems & Craft: a palette with six paints in the site's colors and a brush that sweeps a blend, and five samples of the site's materials fanned out under a loupe.
        - **The strike, on every one:** a line for the stream runs in from the upper left and strikes beside the thing. Shards fly out. One for each part flies out and home and becomes that part, a beat apart. The thing's lights come on last. The other shards fall to the row's border and fade. The whole of it takes about two seconds.
        - Pressing a thing strikes it again. Strike all and half speed are at the foot of the screen. `?at=1.05` holds every strike at that many seconds for a still.
        - What is not right, plainly: these are boxes, rods and discs. None has the detail he asked for, and a rough cannot show it. The paints look pale because they mirror the room. The screen's front cannot be seen in a still. The line for the stream is a flat strip. How the assembly feels in motion was not watched: the web chat draws in software.
        - Stills: `reference/studies/48-skill-things-*.jpg`.
    - **His verdict on the derby (Nov 14), in Settled, Skills:** the CPU in its socket, the screen with its back off, the palette and brush. He is on the fence about the things being made out of the strike's shards, and found the derby too low in quality to show it. He asked whether to go on to Claude Code for a polished example or dial it in here first.
    - **Fable's answer, put to him: go to Claude Code, and prove it on one thing first.** The web chat draws in software and has only a flat strip for the stream, so another round here would be another stand in. In Claude Code the real stream and its shards run on his graphics card. One thing is built first, the CPU in its socket, to the bar, on a page of its own, with three ways of coming to life behind a switch, for him to judge in motion: put together from the strike's shards as proposed. Uncovered by a band of light that crosses it from the strike, as a card in Work is woken. Or lit by the strike's flash with its parts dropping the last short way into place, and no shards turned into parts. The other two things are built after he picks.

31. **Contact and the footer: a wireframe (Nov 14). Four rounds. He approved the fourth, and it is the basic version for the build** (Settled, "Contact, the first build"). Study 26, built in the web chat on his word that Contact is next. It was at `/lab/contact` through its four rounds and is now at `/lab/contact-draft`. What follows is the record of the rounds, newest design last.
    - **His verdict on the first round (Nov 14), in Settled, Contact:** no. The content is one block and not split by the rocket, the stream runs into a pool the rocket launches from and does not strike its nose, and the left column was a mess. The page still shows the first round until the second is drawn.
    - **What Fable put to him the same day, as a brainstorm. None of it is ruled:**
        - What the section is for, in this order: book time with him in one press. Copy his email or write a few lines without the page fighting it. End the page.
        - Fable's read of why the first round failed: the rocket cut one job in two, so the eye had to cross it to get from the calendar button to the form. The left column was five kinds of type with nothing holding them together. A strike on the nose left the stream ending in mid air, and had nothing to do with a launch. A pool has a reason: it is what the rocket burns.
        - **For any layout:**
            1. One block, in the order his live site already uses: the calendar button and his email on one row, "or send a message", the form, then the line for where he is based. One width and one left edge.
            2. The block is one plate, of the same family as the Work cards. A solid edge lets it lie over the scene. The launch's light falls on its rim and not behind words someone is typing. It ties the foot of the page to Work.
            3. The pool is the foot of the page: long and shallow, along the bottom. The stream falls into it. It fills over the last stretch of scroll and is full at the foot of the page, which is when the rocket goes. After the launch it stays lit. It is the page's last picture, with the footer under it.
            4. The rocket stands in the pool at one side and is never behind words.
        - **Three arrangements:**
            - A · Beside. The block at one side, the rocket at the other, both over the one pool, the block's lower edge lying over the pool's end. The launch and the thing he wants a visitor to do are on the last screen together. For the short time the launch runs, the two share the eye.
            - B · Behind. The rocket large, as the ground of the section, with the block laid partly over it. The most depth. It covers half of a piece made to be looked at, puts a launch behind a form, and repeats what About does with his portrait.
            - C · Below. The block centered as his live site has it, and under it the launch as a band of its own across the whole page, the rocket on the center line under the city. Nothing competes with the form and the page ends on the launch alone. The page is a screen longer, and when the rocket goes the form is above the screen.
        - **Fable's pick: A, with the block at the right and the rocket at the left.** The stream leaves About under the lower left of his portrait, so it falls into the pool beside the rocket with nothing carrying it. About has its words at the left and him at the right, so the two sections do not repeat each other.
        - The pool also answers what he had not answered, whether the rocket launches once or every time: the pool drains as the stream draws back, and the rocket is on it again by the time it fills. Fable's proposal.
    - **The derby he asked for is built (Nov 14), second round, same page.** A switch at the foot of the screen sets A · Beside, B · Behind or C · Below. In all three:
        - The block is one plate in his live site's order: the calendar button, his email with a copy button, "or send a message", the form, the line for where he is based.
        - The stream falls into a long pool at the foot of the section. The pool fills as the page is scrolled on and is full at the foot of the page. The rocket lights from its foot up as the pool fills, and launches from the pool when it is full.
        - After the launch the pool stays lit, with the stream still falling into it. Scrolling back up drains the pool and brings the rocket back down.
        - The block and the title come on as the stream passes the block's top. The rocket and the pool come up as the stream nears the pool.
    - **Where each had to give, plainly:**
        - A: nothing had to. The stream falls between the rocket and the block and touches neither. The rocket is drawn a little larger than the carried one's outline, 538 units tall, so it stands as tall as the block.
        - B: the title could not stand over the rocket, so it is inside the plate, smaller. The tower is behind the plate and only its top shows. The stream had to lean about 100 units to the left to land clear of the table.
        - C: the block is in the middle, so the stream passes behind it, as it does behind a card in Work, and the title is inside the plate. The rocket could not stand on the center line, because the stream comes out from under the block there. It is about 100 units to the right. The section is 1390 units tall against A's 830.
    - What is not right, plainly: outlines, a line, a flat panel for the plate and an oval for the pool. How it moves cannot be judged in the web chat. The pool dims while the rocket burns and reads as nearly empty in a still.
    - Stills: `reference/studies/51-contact-*.jpg`.
    - **Does the rocket fit, and what Fable answered (Nov 14). Put to him, nothing ruled:**
        - Yes, if it is made of the page's own metal. A white aerospace rocket would not fit. A rocket in the bronze and copper of the Sun stones and the buttons, riveted and engine turned, is the same world one age on. The page then runs ancient, modern, next: the stones, the desk, the launch.
        - The bridge Fable proposes: the pool is a Sun stone laid flat. A great carved disc in the ground, whose rings and glyphs fill with the stream's light from the middle outward. The fill he asked for becomes the stone lighting ring by ring, and the rocket launches from the oldest thing on the page.
        - The covering's second place: the Contact plate. It sleeps as the engine turned blank with its knob, and wakes by the band of light when the stream reaches the section, as a card does. A pointer or keyboard focus wakes it too, as on a card.
        - Other pieces Fable weighed, each of which goes against the settled line "A rocket launch": a lighthouse, which is an ancient wonder and means a signal, with a quieter ending than a launch. An orrery that the pool sets turning, which fits Work's world and says nothing about reaching him.
        - Fable's pick: keep the rocket, in the page's metal, on a Sun stone pool, with the blank on the plate.
    - **Drawn on his yes (Nov 14), third round, same page.** Arrangement A only. B and C are out of the code, and their stills are `reference/studies/51-contact-*.jpg`.
        - The plate sleeps as the engine turned blank: rays round a knob. When the stream passes its top a band of light crosses it from the corner nearest the stream, the knob draws back ahead of the band, and the form is behind the band. Moving the pointer onto the plate, or tabbing into it, wakes it too. It goes back to sleep when the stream draws back.
        - The pool is a Sun stone laid flat under the rocket: a disc with six rings, studs, ticks and rays cut into it, and a boss at its middle that the rocket's table stands on. The stream lands on its outer band.
        - **It lights from the rim in, not from the middle out as Fable had told him.** The stream lands at the rim, so its light has to travel in to the rocket. The boss is the last to light, and that is when the rocket goes. The page says so.
        - The plate no longer lies over the pool. The stone is round and is seen whole, 60 units clear of the plate.
        - Scrolled back up, the stone goes dark from the middle out before the rocket has landed. It is the stone draining, and it is his to say whether that reads.
    - What is not right, plainly: every part is outline. The stone's carving is a few rings and marks and nothing like the Sun stones in Work. The blank is drawn lines and a drawn knob, not the lit metal of a card. The lit stone is flat color.
    - Stills: `reference/studies/52-contact-*.jpg`.
    - **Fourth round, after his look (Nov 14), same page:**
        - The plate wakes when the stream lands on the stone. Its band sets out from its lower left corner, the corner nearest the landing.
        - **How the screen is seated for the landing.** In this section the stream's end is read from a fixed distance above the foot of the screen, not from two thirds of the way down it. It lands when the foot of the stone is 78 units above the foot of the screen. From the title's top to the stone's foot is 671 units, and the page ends 22 units after the landing. Measured on six screens (1440 by 900, 1366 by 768, 1440 by 790, 1920 by 1080, 1720 by 1300, 1180 by 820): at the landing, and again at the end of the page, the title, the whole plate, the rocket and the stone are inside the screen on every one.
        - Because the page has almost run out by then, the stone no longer fills with the scroll. Once the stream is on it, it lights from its rim to its middle by itself in 2.4 seconds, and then the rocket goes. Fable's call, his to overturn.
        - The footer is 100 units, down from 230, so the end of the page does not push the title off a small screen.
        - "Book on Calendly" on the primary button, his live site's words. "Copy my email" as the secondary button, with no address shown. "Boise, Idaho · Mountain Time" under the form. The tag beside the title is gone.
    - **Fable's answer on the email: keep it, as the secondary button.** The form and the button serve different people. Someone he is interviewing with will often rather write from their own mail, where they have a copy and can add people. A form on a portfolio is the path people trust least. It costs one button. His to cut.
    - **For whoever builds it:** a screen too short to hold 671 units cannot show all of it at once. Under about 700 px tall at a desktop's width, the title leaves the top before the stream lands. What happens there is not designed.
    - Stills: `reference/studies/53-contact-*.jpg`.
    - **What follows is the first round as it was drawn.** It is no longer on the page.
    - **What it holds, from his words (Settled, Contact):** a calendar button first, a way to copy his email, and a working form. Nothing waits for a message.
    - **Fable's calls on what he left open, each his to overturn:**
        - No phone number. Everyone who reads the site already has his résumé, which carries it. A number on a public page is collected by machines and called by strangers, and this repo is public too. The calendar button does what a number is for.
        - One short line for where he is based and his time zone. It tells someone booking a call what hours he keeps, and it is where he can say he would move for the right role. The wording is his. The wireframe shows "City, State · Mountain Time" as a stand in: no place is written in the repo until he gives the line.
        - LinkedIn, GitHub and the résumé go in the footer and are not repeated in Contact. Contact keeps to the three ways to reach him. The live site's footer also links CodePen and YouTube. Whether those stay is his to say.
    - **The layout, Fable's proposal:** the rocket and its tower in the middle of the page, between two columns. At the left: the title, a line, the calendar button, his email address with a button that copies it, and the line for where he is based. At the right: the form (a name, an email, a message, a send button). The launch table stands on the footer's top edge, so the ground the rocket stands on is the line the footer hangs from.
        - Why the middle: the stream leaves About about 40 units left of the center line, so it falls onto the rocket with nothing needed to carry it sideways. The stream begins on the center line too, at the city's lip.
        - Passed over: the rocket at one side and every word at the other. The stream would have had to be carried across the page, over the words.
    - **What the stream does, Fable's proposal. It takes up Open 12:**
        1. The rocket, the tower and the table stand dark. They come up as the stream nears.
        2. The stream falls from under his portrait and strikes the spike on the rocket's nose. It is the one thing it strikes in the section. The words of both columns come on at the strike.
        3. Its light runs down the rocket: the nose, the upper stage and the lower stage light in turn.
        4. It reaches the engine and the engine lights. The tower's arms draw back. A second later the rocket lifts, and climbs the way the stream came down.
        5. Under the rising rocket the stream is its flame. Once the rocket has gone the stream falls on to the empty table and burns there. The page ends on that.
        6. Scrolling back up, the rocket comes back down the stream and lands, the flame goes out, and the section goes dark again. Not ruled. It is what everything else on the page does: what the stream brings on goes away when it draws back.
    - **The form** shows what it says once a message has gone: "Message sent", as the live site has it. Sending a message changes nothing else on the page. In the wireframe it sends nothing.
    - **Not decided, for the build:**
        - How the form sends. The live site posts to a form service straight from the page, with nothing running on a server. Recommendation: keep that, since it works today and asks nothing of him. When sending fails the form says so and points to his email address.
        - The address the copy button copies, and the calendar link. The live site's are known. Neither is written in this repo until he says which to use.
        - What the rocket looks like. Fable reads "the 3D work is made new for this design" (Settled, 3D pieces) as holding for the rocket. He said it of the desk and has not said it of the rocket. The wireframe's outline follows the carried rocket's proportions: about 470 units tall, with its tower at its right.
        - How far up the page the rocket is followed, and whether it is seen crossing the foot of About.
    - **For whoever builds it:**
        - The stream has to reach the engine before the page runs out. The leading end is read two thirds of the way down the screen, so the last third of the last screen is under it. The engine stands 352 units above the foot of the page. That is enough on a screen 900 tall and not on a taller one. The wireframe slides the reading line down the screen over the last half screen of scroll.
        - At the strike, on a screen 900 tall, the top two thirds of both columns are on the screen. The email row, the line for where he is based and the send button are just under it, and are already on when they are scrolled to.
        - While the rocket is in the air nothing of the pad goes dark, however far the stream has drawn back. On a quick scroll up the rocket was still coming down onto a pad that had gone dark.
    - **It leaves out something the carried rocket has:** a flume coming in from the left, which the page sketch (Open 25) brought the stream in along. Here the stream comes in from above and there is no flume. He called what the second build drew inside its pieces, the flume among them, very low quality (The stream).
    - What is not right, plainly: it is outlines and a line. The launch is a few shapes and a glow. Nothing of how it moves can be judged in the web chat. After the launch the middle of the section holds the stream, the tower and a small flame, and whether that is enough of an ending is his to judge once it moves.
    - Stills: `reference/studies/50-contact-*.jpg`.

32. **Phones and an upright iPad: the hero as a picture of the world (Nov 16, in the night). Built by Fable as the first piece of the mobile pass. Not seen by him, not approved, and nothing in it is a rule.** Under 1100 px the hero was his words and nothing else, and on the whole page the stream came in from the top and ran through his name.
    - **What is built:** under his words a narrow screen shows the world as one picture (`STILLS` in `src/config/stills.ts`, taken by `npm run still` from the real hero page with the stream fallen from the core, 130 KB, never fetched by a wide screen). On a phone it is wider than the screen, centred on the spire, so the city and the core are large enough to read and the pyramid's far foot and the dunes' far end are off it. On an upright iPad the whole planet shows. It fades into the page at its top, sides and foot.
    - **Skills the same way:** over the rows a narrow screen shows the desk as one picture, lit, with the section's title on its floor as on the live desk (39 KB). The heading stays in the page for a screen reader and is not drawn twice.
    - **The stream:** it begins where the picture's own stream fades out, comes on by light, and falls into Work (`NARROW` in `src/whole/hub.ts`), so the picture's stream and the page's read as one. Work no longer stands a whole empty screen under the hero. Its two sides end beside Work's button: run on to the foot of the page they crossed the words of every section under it.
    - **Why a picture and not the live world:** a phone's browser ends a page for memory far sooner than his Mac, and the live world with the stream holds over a gigabyte in WebKit. The picture costs nothing to draw and loads at once. It does not move: the debris does not turn and the cursor's light is not there.
    - **Fable recommends** keeping it for phones, and asking of the mobile pass whether an upright iPad gets the live world instead.
    - **Work on a phone (Nov 16, about 05:50):** the cards were 243 px wide on a 390 px phone, narrower than their own foot, so every card with three links ran out of its plate and under the stream. A card is now as wide as the screen leaves it (290 px there) and every card is one size, the foot closes up under 290 px so it stays one row, and each side of the stream moves out under the title and falls clear of the bar and the cards, about 22 px in from the screen's edge (`narrow` in `STREAM.work`), to meet on the button.
    - **About on a phone:** his portrait stands under the title with its light behind it, the tabs lie over its fading foot, and the words come after. Under the words, as it was, his forearms ended in the air over most of a screen of empty ground.
    - **Contact on a phone:** the two buttons are as wide as the plate, a name and an email are one above the other, and under the plate the launch pad stands lit as one picture (36 KB), the page's last image.
    - **Work's category bar under 1100 px (Nov 16, about 06:00):** one row of four, as on a wide screen, each word as wide as it is and the type scaled to the screen, from a 320 px phone up. It was two rows of two, a block of words twice the bar's height (his words of Nov 13: "the category selection for one is really bad looks").
    - **An upright iPad and Contact's plate (Nov 16, about 07:00):** About and Contact are each one column a person can read in the middle of the screen. Under 1100 px Contact's plate is a plate in plain paint, a dark bronze face with a bead of copper along its upper edge, where it was a bare outline.
    - **The stream on a narrow screen (Nov 16, about 15:00):** it strikes and wakes Work's cards and comes back at Contact to launch the rocket, live (Settled, Hero, "His look at the whole page on his Mac", point 8, "Built").
    - **Still to design in the mobile pass, none of it started:** whether an upright iPad gets the live world and the live desk in place of their pictures, and the rest of point 8 for it. Open 18 stands.

33. **The All work page (Nov 17). Being designed with him, nothing agreed yet.** "We should talk about the all work page and come up with the design and a build plan for that. I want to review what you recommend, approve it and maybe even tweak it, and we'll work together on it to make sure it looks great and up to par with the rest of the site."
    - **What is already settled about it** (Settled, Work): "A View more button goes to an All work page with the same categories, showing everything. There are no separate pages for games, photography or 3D art." The hero's and Work's View all work buttons go to `/work`, which is a plain placeholder today.
    - **What is known:** his live site's All Work page lists 8 projects, all software: the four on the home page, and Modern Softworks, Hero Animation Builder, Meridian and Micro-interactions Library. Games, 3D art and Photography have nothing real yet.
    - **His answers (Nov 17). Two rulings, and the rest being talked through:**
        - **Every category shows, with placeholder cards. A ruling.** "I actually will have projects for all the categories, so for now let's just build it with those placeholder cards and at the end of development I will add in the actual real projects with the screenshots and all that, so no reason to hide anything."
        - **Two columns, not three. A ruling.** "I will take your recommendation on that and not do it."
        - **Sub categories, raised and not ruled.** "It may just be a lot of projects for software unless we want to add in sub categories as well, which I believe we had talked about, but never implemented. By sub categories I mean like software and then another menu appears where you can select AI or Three.js or something. I can't remember what we talked about." Nothing in this brief settles them: the nearest is "a second bar under WORK" in a list of things not built.
        - **A project's own page is wanted, and the live site's is not the model.** "We also need another page and that's when you actually click on a project and get all the information about the project. The one on my current portfolio was a bit lackluster. It didn't have enough data points to really explain what this is, why we built it, the technologies that are involved with it, a place for the screenshots, place for the video, a good place for the links." (Settled already: "Project pages hold video, click through screenshots, clear live and code links, more detail, and the year.")
        - **The stream on these pages is not required, and the layout comes first.** "I'm not opposed to just not including the stream on these sub pages. If you think we should for consistency I'm totally open with that. I think right now I just kinda wanna get the layout and everything put together and then we could figure out the flare, the 3D objects or whatever we're gonna put on the page to make it look even better."
        - **Where he leans for the look of these pages.** "I think I'm more into atmospherical, environmental type stuff for the background, to make it feel like we're in a 3D space, utilizing really good high quality light to create shadows and depth."
        - He asked for Fable's thoughts on all of it. They are in `LOG.md`, top entry, Nov 17.
    - **Approved to build, as a first version to iterate on (Nov 17).** "Everything you have here looks good for now. Let's just go ahead and build it. We'll probably iterate on it and make some changes, but I'll have to get a visual on it to know for sure, so you have my permission to go ahead and put these in the pipeline." What that covers, as Fable put it to him: no stream on All work or on a project's page, every card awake. Sub categories later, as a row of filter words, with the room left for it. A project's page in ten parts (head, facts plate, lead media, what it is, why I built it, how it works, what it proved, built with, gallery, foot). Ask the Claude Docs as the model. The atmosphere chosen later from a derby. None of this is a ruling on the look: he judges it from the built pages.
    - **His look at the first versions (Nov 17). Rulings:**
        - **All work's layout is good.** "I think this looks really good." "Looking at the all work page it looks good."
        - **Sub categories go in. A ruling.** "We should definitely add in the sub categories." Software has them. "I'm not sure if 3D art and games need them. I suppose we could put some on 3D art where we could explain like if this is something that's printable or a game asset or whatever, and then for games we could do like web, Steam, Unreal, Unity, something like that, whatever makes sense." **Built the same hour:** a row of quiet words under the bar (All and the category's sub categories), which shows only what is under the one picked, with the count following it. Games: Web, Steam, Unreal, Unity. 3D art: Printable, Game asset. Software: AI, 3D, Design systems, Tools, which are Fable's proposal, as is which project is under which (`SUBS` and `UNDER` in `src/content/work.ts`). A project may be under more than one.
        - **Photography has no sub categories, and a card there is a shoot. A ruling.** "I'm not sure if photography needs one. I was thinking more like a gallery: you click on it and it might take you to a page where I'm kind of just explaining the photo shoot or whatever, and then you could just click through a handful of the high quality pictures. So the layouts for the project pages may actually be slightly different." Not built: a shoot's page is a second kind of project page, to be designed.
        - **The project page he saw was his live site's.** "For the actual case study pages this looks just like my existing website, oh I just realized it is lol." Seven of the eight cards still go there. Only Ask the Claude Docs opens the new page, and Fable pointed him to it.

That means reviewing the mobile, tablet, desktop, 4K versions of the site, each section, making sure they're all perfect. Doing an audit on performance, user experience." "All of this has to be planned out and really thought about beforehand, so I'm just gonna let you get to work. Feel free to utilize agents in any workflows that you need to. When you report back to me, everything should be pretty much complete and ready for my review and deployment. Besides maybe hooking up the contact form we should be all good."
    - **The navigation could be better.** "Let's take a look at the navigation. I think that could be a bit better. I know it's super simple and that's what I asked for, but like maybe a background or something would add to it."

    - **The city is blurry.** "I will say the city is a bit blurry now. I understand we probably had to do that for performance reasons, but I would love to make it look better while keeping performance."
    - **The debris field lags.** "The asteroid field still is a bit laggy even on desktop sometimes." And what it is to him: "In my opinion, the exploded part of the planet in the asteroid field is the coolest part of the site."
    - **More debris, in more places.** "I also wouldn't be opposed to adding more of like the asteroids. I know I keep calling them asteroids, but like the debris, even in more places around the site, like down around the contact section and the spaceship. I think it would just add some consistency and some really coolness to it."
    - **More texture.** "I'm also open to adding more like textures around the places like our work pages."
    - **The footer, designed by Fable for him to judge (Nov 18). Not ruled.** One foot under every page, outside the page's main block (`src/components/SiteFoot.tsx`): at the left his name, his line from the hero and his city. At the right two short lists, Site (Home, All work, Contact) and Elsewhere (LinkedIn, GitHub, Résumé). Under a hairline, "Designed and built by Brandon Church · © 2026" and a way back to the top. No box and no band: it opens with a rule of copper that dies away to each side. On the home page it replaces the single line that stood inside Contact, and it is sized on the page's board so it grows with the screen. The labels Site, Elsewhere, Contact and Back to top are stand ins.
    - **Debris round the launch pad, designed by Fable and with an Opus builder (Nov 18).** The page opens with what broke away under the planet and closes with the same rock hanging round the pad, lit by the stone and by the rocket's fire: `reference/kits/pad-debris.md`. Other places are not built: Fable wants him to see this one first.
    - **"More textures around the places like our work pages" can be read two ways, and Fable has asked him which.** More texture on the work pages themselves, or the kind of texture the work pages have (the plastered wall under one raking light) in other places, such as behind the home page's sections. Not built until he says: the second costs a full screen canvas on the page that already holds the most memory on his iPad.
    - **What the audits and the builders found about his two complaints of speed, and what was done (Nov 18):**
        - **"The city is a bit blurry."** On a desktop at its own zoom the world was already drawn at the screen's full density. It is soft on an iPad, where the canvas gets about one pixel to a CSS pixel on a screen that has two (Safari ends the page for memory otherwise), and on a zoomed desktop. Built: the unchanging city (the stone and windows of every tower and of the spire, the land, the dunes) is drawn once at the screen's density into a plain picture under the world's canvas, for about 37 MB. **Still soft on an iPad: the gold and the trees**, which change with the cursor's light and cannot be kept.
        - **"The asteroid field is a bit laggy."** It was not the field. Skills' and Contact's 3D pieces were being built from five screens away, which on the whole page is while the visitor scrolls the hero. Built: on a desktop every piece is made up front behind the preloader, and none is let go. A real scroll of the whole page at 600 px a second went from about 17 frames over 20 ms, the worst 100 to 150 ms, to 1, the worst 33. **What it costs:** the preloader shows for about two seconds longer on a desktop. An iPad is as it was.
    - **In exact terms:** one pass over the whole site, planned in `PLAN.md` ("The finishing pass"), in which Fable does the creative work (the navigation, the footer, where debris and texture go), Opus builders make the contained things from briefs, and fresh Opus auditors check every page at a phone, a tablet upright and sideways, a desktop and 4K, its speed, its memory and its use. What is his alone stays his: the words, the real pictures, the domain, and hooking up the form.

            - The stutter is much less and not gone: 8 or 9 frames over 33 ms in a scroll from top to foot where there were 31 to 46, the worst about 70 ms where it was 150 to 220.
            - Fable's recommendation, which went against **"Nothing ships over the memory rule on an iPad held sideways"** by about 150 MB at Contact: the world and About's two things as pictures there, with the sky, the stream and the rocket live, which is what an upright iPad already holds or less. **He ruled for it on Nov 21: the next bullet.**
    - **An iPad holds about 1150 at most on the way down, with a net under it. A ruling (Nov 21). It replaces "Nothing ships over the memory rule on an iPad held sideways" for iPads.** "Option 2, plus a net, the same line for an upright iPad, and a switch for my own iPad." "An iPad holds about 1150 at most on the way down, with the net under it. That replaces 'nothing ships over the memory rule' for iPads. A phone and a desktop stay as they are." In exact terms, as he gave them:
        - **The line.** On an iPad, laid out wide or narrow, the most the home page holds on the way down (`npm run mem -- / --profile`, GPU, MB, Safari's engine) is about 1150. A phone keeps the rule of about 1000, which it is inside. A desktop is as it is.
        - **1. An iPad laid out wide (from 1100 px): the world and About's two things are pictures.** Now: the world, the reflector and the studio light are live there. To be: each is a picture. The sky, the stream and the rocket stay live. "Build the world's picture properly, with the stream still leaving the core and debris that moves, and show it to me before it is the default." So until he has seen it and said yes, it is behind a switch and a wide iPad draws what it draws today.
        - **2. An iPad laid out narrow (under 1100 px): the most it holds is brought down to the same line by letting far cards go sooner.** Now: a card more than a screen and a half away holds nothing, and the page holds about 1305 four screens down. "Look at a fast flick through Work in real frames first. If a card can be seen being made, stop and tell me."
        - **3. The net.** "If Safari ends the page while it is on the screen, it comes back as the plain page (?plain=1) and stays plain on that device." To be proved "in Safari on this Mac by killing the page's process".
        - **4. `?live=1`, remembered on the device, shows an iPad everything live as it was before the pictures.** "It is for my own iPad. The net still applies to it."
        - **Measured:** both layouts on a production build with `npm run mem`, `--profile` and `--walk`, giving loaded, the most and the foot.
        - What this changes on an iPad laid out wide, once he has approved the picture: the world's debris no longer moves as the live field does and the core no longer pulses as the live one does, except by what the picture is given, and the reflector and the studio light no longer take the cursor's light.
        - **Built (Nov 21, Claude Code). `LOG.md`, Nov 21, "An iPad at about 1150 with a net under it", has every figure. Two things wait on him: the world's picture, and an iPad laid out narrow.**
            - **His verdict (Nov 21, 13:45), to the web chat with Claude Code's report: "I did the testing on my end and honestly everything looks great to me."** He had been asked to open `?world=picture` on his iPad and scroll through the hero, and to press a category on his Mac with the stream on the first row only. Read as, and put to him the same minute: **yes to the world's picture**, which becomes the default on an iPad laid out wide (`world` joins `HANDHELD.pictures`: for Claude Code, not yet done), and **the second row's fade is right** (Open 37). **He did not rule on an iPad laid out narrow.** Fable's recommendation there, said to him in bold as going against "an iPad holds about 1150 at most": the gentler way switched on (`?put=0.25`, about 1220 for a moment) and the rest left to the net. Cutting Work to Software's four cards would reach the line, and whether the three stand in categories stay is still his to say.
            - **1. The world's picture is built and is not the default: `?world=picture` shows it on any device.** The planet is one picture. What broke away is 91 groups of its own pieces, each drifting as the live family does (rise, slide, swing round a great piece, the arms swinging round the stream), with the chips the stream carries falling down it and shards riding the neck of light. The stream's light on the rock, the neck and the pieces beside it follows the stream's leading end, as on the live world. The page's stream is live and leaves the core as it always does. What it does not have: no piece tumbles, the core's grain does not turn, and the cursor's glint is gone. Beside the live world: `reference/studies/75-the-hero-world-live-and-as-pictures.jpg`. It is his to see moving, on his iPad, before it is the default.
            - **About's two things are pictures on an iPad laid out wide now**, each as two, without the stream on it and with (`reference/studies/75-abouts-two-things-as-pictures.jpg`). The reflector no longer nods when it is struck.
            - **Measured with the world as its pictures** (production build, Safari's engine as a sideways iPad, GPU, MB): loaded 690 to 770, the most on the way down 1148 to 1173, the foot 1146 to 1194, and 332 to 345 still held after leaving. The most is at Contact. **About 1150, and over it by up to 40 in some runs.** As it ships until he approves the picture (the world live): loaded 1170 to 1310, the most 1585 to 1592, the foot 1396 to 1464.
            - **2. An iPad laid out narrow is not changed, and is not at the line: he asked to be told.** It holds 1318 to 1338 for a moment four screens down (loaded 644 to 676, the foot 940 to 981). A card can be seen being made: with every far card let go from 0.75 of a screen, a fast flick back through Work shows a card with only its words for 0.15 to 0.4 seconds, and the most it holds is still 1188 to 1197. A gentler way was built and measured, letting only the cards that are put away go sooner (twelve of Work's sixteen): 1211 to 1232, and no showing card is made late in a flick, but a press followed within a tenth of a second by a fast flick shows a quarter of a card unmade for three or four frames. Both together: 1187. **Neither reaches 1150.** Both are switches (`?plates=0.75`, `?put=0.25`) and neither is on.
            - **A fault found on the way and mended:** a fast flick already showed a card being made at today's distance, for up to half a second, because the twelve cards that are put away were made ahead of the four on the screen. What shows is made first now: at 9000 px a second no card is seen unmade, and on the fastest flicks one is for about 0.12 to 0.15 seconds.
            - **3. The net is built and proved in Safari on his Mac.** The page's process was ended while the page was on the screen. Safari loaded it again within a second, with its banner "This webpage was reloaded because a problem occurred", and it came back plain. It stayed plain on the next load, and in a new tab. `?plain=0` brought it back live. It is under a handheld only: a desktop is never held plain. (`reference/studies/77-the-net-in-safari-the-page-back-plain.jpg`)
            - **4. `?live=1` is built**, remembered on the device until `?live=0`: every piece live, none a picture. Measured, it holds what an iPad held before the pictures: 2181 at the most. The net is under it, and after the net has held a device plain, `?live=1` again (or `?plain=0`) lets it go.
            - **A phone is as it was and inside its rule:** loaded 502, the most 858, the foot 813.
        - **His rulings on what waited on him (Nov 21, on reading the report):**
            - **The world's picture is approved, and is the default on an iPad laid out wide.** "The world's picture: yes. Make it the default on an iPad laid out wide." `?world=live` still shows the live world there, and `?live=1` everything live.
            - **A narrow iPad holds about 1220 for a moment.** "An iPad laid out narrow: turn the gentler way on and leave the rest to the net. Write it into BRIEF.md as my ruling: a narrow iPad holds about 1220 for a moment." In exact terms: on an iPad laid out narrow (under 1100 px) the cards that are put away, twelve of Work's sixteen, hold nothing unless they are within a quarter of a screen of the screen. The cards that show are as they were. The line for that layout is about 1220 at the most on the way down, where every other iPad's is about 1150, and the net is under it. What it costs, which he was told: a press followed within a tenth of a second by a fast flick shows a quarter of a card unmade for three or four frames.
            - **Built and measured again (Nov 21, Claude Code), as it now ships.** An iPad laid out wide, with the world as its pictures by default: loaded 680 to 750, the most 1135 and 1175, the foot 1127 to 1170, and 330 still held after leaving. An iPad laid out narrow: loaded 568 to 606, the foot 958 to 1044, 329 after leaving, and **the most 1211 to 1270 over seven runs (1211, 1224, 1232, 1256, 1266, 1269, 1270). His figure of about 1220 came from the first two: it is about 1250, and up to 50 over his line.** Said to him. With the cards that are put away held only while their own place is on the screen (`?put=0`) it is 1196 and 1198: not on, his to say.
            - **A narrow iPad holds about 1250 for a moment, with the net under it. A ruling (Nov 21). It replaces about 1220.** "A narrow iPad: keep it as it is. Write it into BRIEF.md as my ruling: about 1250 for a moment, with the net under it. That replaces about 1220." In exact terms: an iPad laid out narrow stays as built (the cards that are put away held only within a quarter of a screen of the screen, `HANDHELD.put`), its line is about 1250 at the most on the way down, and the stricter setting (`?put=0`) is not turned on.
37. **The sleeping blanks come off the home page, and are kept (Nov 20, 15:39). His word.** "As much as I hate to do this, I think I want to remove the placeholder cards that are on top of the work projects and the contact form from the landing page. I feel like they're just not needed. I wanna keep that asset as I may want to use it in the future but for now I want to disable it."
    - **In exact terms (Fable's reading, put to him the same minute, his to correct):**
        - "The placeholder cards" are the engine turned blank with its knob: what a Work card is while the stream is not on it, and what Contact's plate is until the stream lands on the stone.
        - Now: on the home page a Work card is the blank until the stream strikes it, or a pointer or keyboard focus is put on it, and it goes back to the blank when the stream draws back above it. Contact's plate, with its title, buttons and form, is the blank until the stream lands.
        - To be: on the home page, at every width, every Work card shows its picture and its words from the start and never becomes the blank. Contact's plate, its title, its buttons and its form are there from the start. No band of light, no waking, no going back to sleep.
        - Kept: the blank, its knob and the band of light stay in the code and working, behind one switch. `/lab/work-blank` still shows them, and `?blanks=1` turns them back on for a look at any page. Nothing of them is deleted.
    - **What this sets aside while the switch is off. Set aside, not dropped: each stands again the moment he turns the blanks back on.**
        - The stream, "The fourth pass", items 5 to 8: "A card is asleep whenever the stream is not on it", the blank as what a sleeping card is, the band as the change between asleep and awake, and "On a press, a row the stream has not reached does nothing". With them, "A card sleeps again", "A sleeping card is a blank", "The band, mended" and the rules for a pointer or keyboard focus waking a card.
        - Contact, "Contact, the first build", items 8 to 10 ("Asleep it is the engine turned blank with its knob", "It wakes by the one band of light", "A pointer put on it, or keyboard focus inside it, wakes it"), and "The plate wakes when the stream lands on the pool under the rocket".
        - The tests for the blank and the band in `QUALITY.md` are for the blanks switched on.
    - **What stands again as first written:** "A card is always readable. The stream's strike lights it and never hides it" (Cards and buttons), and `QUALITY.md`'s "The card is never dark waiting for the stream". The Nov 13 change of that line to "readable once the stream has reached it" holds only with the blanks on.
    - **What does not change:**
        - The stream still strikes every card it reaches: the rim flares, shards are thrown, and the picture is left as it was. The stones, the rail, the button and the surge are as they are.
        - A card under the pointer still lights, turns and grows as he picked, and on a touch screen larger than a phone a first tap still lights a card and a second opens it.
        - In Contact the stone still lights when the stream lands, the rocket still launches, and the screen is still seated for the landing.
        - Skills and About still come onto the page with the stream. He did not name them.
    - **One thing comes back with it, said to him.** The blanks were the answer to Open 21: a category pressed while the stream has not reached the second row, with those two cards on the screen (his words then: "looks horrible", and "when the light's not touching them ... that animation does not play at all"). With the blanks off those cards are awake and have to change with the stream not on them.
        - **Fable's call, his to overturn, and it goes against "On a press, a row the stream has not reached does nothing":** a card the stream is not on changes in place by a short plain fade, in its row's turn, top down. No drop, no rise, no strike, no flash, so the surge's animation still never plays where the light is not touching. Cards the stream is on keep the surge exactly as it is.
        - It is motion, so it is judged on his Mac. He rules on it once he has pressed the bar with the second row not reached.
    - **Fable's view, said to him:** it is the right call for a page whose job is to show his work, since every project and the form are seen at once by someone who scrolls fast or lands part way down. What it costs is the wake as a moment, and the press case above.
    - **Built (Nov 21, an Opus builder from the prompt, read and brought in by Fable). His to judge moving.** `BLANKS` in `src/config/site.ts` is off, and `?blanks=1` in a page's address turns the blanks back on for that page. `/lab/work-blank` is as it was. Checked in real frames on a production build at a desktop, a sideways iPad and a phone: no card is ever the blank, Contact's plate, title, buttons and form are there before the stream lands, the stone still lights and the rocket still launches.
    - **What a press does now to a card the stream has not reached** (`reference/studies/76-a-press-with-the-second-row-not-reached.jpg`): it changes in place by a crossfade of about a quarter of a second, in its row's turn on the surge's own timeline (on a desktop the first row from about 0.12 s after the press, the second from about 0.58 s). No drop, no rise, no strike, no flash. Cards the stream is on keep the surge as it was. **One thing he may read as the surge touching the second row:** where the surge's front catches the stream's leading end beside that row, the end bursts, as it always has. The cards themselves get no strike. Not ruled.
    - **His verdict (Nov 21, 13:45): the fade is right.** "I did the testing on my end and honestly everything looks great to me." And to Claude Code: "The second row's fade is right." Said after pressing a category on his Mac with the second row not reached. This rules Fable's call above, and "On a press, a row the stream has not reached does nothing" stays set aside while the blanks are off.

- Hexagons, thin plates and panes, and chips of the letters as the shape of the shards. He picked facets (Nov 12)
- Chunky cut facets with detail on their surface. He picked the jagged look (Nov 12)
- The client work section
- The process section
- The "More rooms" section and the separate room pages

- Whole brass plates as cards
- A card's picture cast as a relief in metal, or etched as lines (Nov 13)
- A streak or spot of the cursor's light on a card's picture (Nov 13)
- A metal bar over WORK, or under it. "I don't want the bar above work" (Nov 13)
- A card's colors coming back as a circle spreading from the pointer (Nov 13)
- Doors as the sleeping card: two mirrored doors that meet at a seam with a latch. "Let's cut C. I don't love that idea" (Nov 13)
- For the sleeping card's blank: the card's layout etched as hairlines, the layout as a mould of pockets, a face cut into facets, and a line and a sun cut into polished bronze. He picked the engine turned dial (Nov 13)
- A change of course that rises to a point and drives off, C in the path derby. "I definitely don't like the point shoot" (Nov 13)
- Cards of different sizes, and a second row of tags on a card (Nov 13)
- The rows of skills in a column beside the desk, as the mirror of About. He picked the rows under the desk (Nov 14)
- In About: the stream falling between the words and his portrait, and the tabs standing on a bar the stream runs along. He wants it behind his portrait and clear of the tabs (Nov 14)
- For the desk: B · Bureau, an antique writing table, and C · Rail, a knife edged slab on steel frames with Work's rail along the front. He picked A · Bench (Nov 14)
- All four cards changing at once when the category changes, with nothing travelling. A surge of 0.45 or 0.7 seconds. He picked 0.7 from the wireframe, saw it on the real page, and changed to 1.1 (Nov 13)
- For the card: its colors coming back as a sweep, lights first, or with the picture coming closer. The card leaning toward the cursor. The thin bead as its edge. He picked the fade, leaning away and the deep edge (Nov 13)
- The faint channel drawn ahead of the stream
- Molten metal as what the stream is made of. Agreed and then set aside for a core of light with shards (Nov 11)
- Water, golden water and lava as what the stream is made of (Nov 11). Light and electricity were dropped the same day and came back when he asked to see a core of light and an arc
- Threads and Ink as what the stream is. He picked Copper leaf (Nov 11)
- Scales, bearings and parts as what the stream is (Nov 11)
- An arc as the whole core of the stream. He picked the wave of light. The arc stays where A has it, at the leading end (Nov 11)
- A permanent grid floor
- Skills tied to desk objects, and skills in narrow pillars
- The game board and the orrery
- The sculpture as built
- A gallery with his work on walls
- Figures that light up on scroll. The Sun stones' faces are the one exception: he saw them lit and said they look good (Nov 13)
- The "laid back, fun to try" voice
- Three hovers for the buttons: light running round the border, Heat and Pull. He chose Catch (Nov 11)
- The second build's kit, its screenshot gates and its voice rules. None of them apply here.
- For the rows of skills: the CPU opened up, a tower in section, and samples under a loupe. He picked the CPU in its socket, the screen with its back off, and the palette and brush (Nov 14)
- In About: his portrait as a figure beside the words. He picked his portrait as the background (Nov 14)
- In Skills: Product Leadership as a fourth skill set, and the numbers on the rows (Nov 14)
- In About: three tabs (about me, what I want to do, my plans for the future). He wants two, Professional and Personal (Nov 14)
- In About: the stream going in behind the shoulder at the left of the picture. He meant his own left (Nov 14)
- In the rows of skills: the sentence of proof in a middle column, and the line under each skill set's name. "It doesn't add anything." (Nov 14)
- In About: the rail ending over his head, with the stream falling off its end into the top of his head. "I definitely don't like the stream coming in directly over my head." (Nov 14)
- In Contact: the content in two columns either side of the rocket. "I don't like the content being separated." (Nov 14)
